=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
Takahashi plans to participate in N AtCoder contests.
In the i-th contest (1 \leq i \leq N), if his rating is between L_i and R_i (inclusive), his rating increases by 1.
You are given Q queries in the following format:

- An integer X is given. Assuming that Takahashi's initial rating is X, determine his rating after participating in all N contests.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
L_1 R_1
L_2 R_2
\vdots
L_N R_N
Q
\text{query}_1
\text{query}_2
\vdots
\text{query}_Q

Here, \text{query}_i is the i-th query in the form:
X

Output

Print Q lines. The i-th line should contain the answer to the i-th query.

Constraints


- 1 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq L_i \leq R_i \leq 5 \times 10^5 (1 \leq i \leq N)
- 1 \leq Q \leq 3 \times 10^5
- For each query, 1 \leq X \leq 5 \times 10^5.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

5
1 5
1 3
3 6
2 4
4 7
3
3
2
5

Sample Output 1

6
6
8

For the 1st query, the rating changes as follows:

- In the 1st contest, the rating is between 1 and 5, so it increases by 1, becoming 4.  
- In the 2nd contest, the rating is not between 1 and 3, so it remains 4.  
- In the 3rd contest, the rating is between 3 and 6, so it increases by 1, becoming 5.  
- In the 4th contest, the rating is not between 2 and 4, so it remains 5.  
- In the 5th contest, the rating is between 4 and 7, so it increases by 1, becoming 6.

For the 2nd query, the rating increases in the 1st, 2nd, 3rd, and 5th contests, ending at 6.
For the 3rd query, the rating increases in the 1st, 3rd, and 5th contests, ending at 8.

Sample Input 2

10
1 1999
1 1999
1200 2399
1 1999
1 1999
1 1999
2000 500000
1 1999
1 1999
1600 2799
7
1
1995
2000
2399
500000
2799
1000

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Segment tree over array `a[0..Q-1]` nondecreasing. Need:
- range_add(l,r,delta)
- find_first_ge(L): smallest index i with a[i] >= L, or Q if none.
- find_last_le(R): largest index i with a[i] <= R, or -1 if none.

Since array is nondecreasing, we can implement these using segment tree storing max and min. With lazy propagation.

Recursive implementation:

```
class SegTree:
    def __init__(self, arr):
        self.n = len(arr)
        size = 4*n
        self.mn = [0]*size
        self.mx = [0]*size
        self.lazy = [0]*size
        build(1,0,n-1)
    def build(node,l,r):
        if l==r: mn=mx=arr[l]
        else...
    def apply(node, val):
        mn[node]+=val; mx[node]+=val; lazy[node]+=val
    def push(node):
        if lazy[node]:
            apply(node*2,lazy[node]); apply(node*2+1,lazy[node]); lazy[node]=0
    def range_add(node,l,r,ql,qr,val):
        if ql<=l and r<=qr: apply; return
        push; mid; recurse; pull
    def first_ge(node,l,r,val):
        if mx[node] < val: return -1? Need return n if none.
        if l==r: return l
        push? For search, we need children mx up-to-date. If we maintain mx with lazy applied to node, children may have pending lazy if node has lazy. When descending, we must push node's lazy to children to ensure child mx correct. Or we can pass accumulated lazy? Simpler push before descending. But push modifies children and clears lazy. This is okay.
        if mx[left] >= val: return first_ge(left)
        else: return first_ge(right)
    def last_le(node,l,r,val):
        if mn[node] > val: return -1
        if l==r: return l
        push
        if mn[right] <= val: return last_le(right)
        else: return last_le(left)
```

Need be careful with `first_ge`: if mx[node] < val, return n. But in recursive function, if no element, return n. Similarly last_le return -1.

Range add after finding l,r: if l<=r.

Since after all operations, we can collect final array by DFS pushing lazy, or point query each rank. Q=3e5, point query O(log) each => 5.7M calls, okay. But we can also do a final traversal to propagate all lazy and fill array in O(Q). Simpler: after processing all intervals, perform a recursive `collect(node,l,r)` that pushes lazy and if leaf sets ans. But recursion over 4Q nodes maybe okay. Or point query each original query using segment tree. Since we need answer in original order, we can store `rank_of_original` and call point_query. Q=3e5, 19 levels each => 5.7M recursive calls, okay. But we can avoid by maintaining an array `val_at_pos`? Range adds lazy, so not directly.

Let's explore sqrt decomposition.

We have array `a` of length Q sorted. Partition into B blocks of size S ~ 700. Number blocks ~ Q/S ~ 430. For each block, store `mn`, `mx`, `lazy` (add to all elements in block). Since array sorted globally, but within a block sorted too. Range add [l,r] by 1:
- If l,r in same block: push lazy to individual array, add to a[l..r], recompute block mn/mx.
- Else: partial left block, full blocks, partial right block. For full blocks, lazy +=1, mn+=1, mx+=1. For partial, push and update elements.
Finding first index with a[i] >= L:
- Iterate blocks from left to right. If block.mx < L, skip. Else within that block, need first position with value >= L. Since block is sorted (after push? If block has lazy, individual values not updated but actual = a[i]+lazy. The block's underlying `a` is sorted; adding lazy preserves order. We can binary search on actual values: need first i in block with a[i]+lazy >= L. Since `a` sorted, binary search over block. We can use bisect on `a` block with key? Python bisect doesn't support key; can compute threshold = L - lazy and bisect_left(block_arr, threshold). Because a[i] + lazy >= L iff a[i] >= L - lazy. So use bisect_left. Good.
- But if we skip blocks using mx, we need ensure mx reflects actual max = block_arr[-1]+lazy. Yes.
Similarly last index with a[i] <= R:
- Iterate blocks from right to left. If block.mn > R, skip. Else within block, need last position with a[i]+lazy <= R iff a[i] <= R - lazy. Use bisect_right(block_arr, R-lazy)-1.
This is O(num_blocks) per boundary if we scan. With ~430 blocks and N=2e5, scanning all blocks for each contest => 86M block checks, maybe okay? But plus updates. Could optimize by using block-level binary search? Since blocks have mn/mx nondecreasing? The block's mn is first element, mx last. Because global array sorted, block.mn and block.mx are nondecreasing across blocks. Thus we can binary search over blocks to find first block with mx >= L, and last block with mn <= R. Then within block use bisect. This gives O(log num_blocks + log S) per boundary. Range add full blocks O(num_blocks) if we update each full block lazy individually; but number of full blocks can be large (up to 430) per operation, N=2e5 => 86M block lazy updates, maybe okay? Actually 2e5*430=86M, each simple, maybe okay in Py? Could be borderline but maybe acceptable. But segment tree is better.

However, sqrt decomposition code is simpler and avoids recursion. Let's evaluate performance. Q=3e5, S=700, num_blocks=429. For each contest:
- find l: binary search over blocks using mx array (list) -> ~9 iterations, each check. Then bisect in block O(log S) ~10.
- find r: binary search over blocks using mn -> ~9.
- range add: if l and r span many blocks, update full blocks. In worst case interval covers all blocks, update 429 blocks each contest => 85.8M lazy increments. Plus partial updates. 85.8M simple Python loop iterations may be ~3-5 seconds? Maybe okay but with other overhead maybe high. Segment tree range add visits O(log Q) nodes, much faster. But sqrt might pass if time limit 2 sec? Unknown. Need robust.

But then complexity similar. Or use block-level lazy and update full blocks in a loop; 86M is high but maybe still okay in PyPy? We need final solution likely expected O((N+Q) log Q), so segment tree is safer.

Let's think about edge cases:
- If no query rating in [L,R], l > r. Then skip.

Now, is the invariant "sequence sorted by current rating" always true if we only process queries (not all possible initial ratings)? We have one token per query. Initially sorted by initial X. Operation increments ratings of tokens whose current rating in [L,R]. We find contiguous segment in sorted order. But what if there are gaps in ratings and tokens with same rating? The segment from first >=L to last <=R includes all tokens with rating in [L,R] because sorted. Yes.

Let's prove with duplicates and ties:
Let array a[0..Q-1] nondecreasing. Let p = first index with a[p] >= L. If none, no token. Let q = last index with a[q] <= R. If q < p, no token. For all i<p, a[i] < L (since a[p] first >=L). For all i>q, a[i] > R (since a[q] last <=R). For p<=i<=q, L <= a[i] <= R. After add 1, for i<p: a[i] <= L-1. For i=p: new a[p] >= L+1. So i<p <= L-1 < L+1 <= new a[p]. For i=q: new a[q] <= R+1. For i=q+1: old a[q+1] >= R+1 (because if a[q+1] <=R then q not last). So new a[q] <= R+1 <= old a[q+1]. Thus sorted. Good.

Now, segment tree search functions rely on array sorted. But after range add, array remains sorted, so min/max of segments reflect sorted order. For `first_ge`, using max of node: if max < val, no element >= val in node. Since array sorted, if max >= val, there is. To find first, go left if left.max >= val else right. This works even with lazy? Need push before checking children because children mx may not include parent's lazy. If parent has lazy, its mx includes lazy, but children mx not. When descending, we must push parent's lazy to children so their mx updated. Alternatively, we can pass an accumulated add value down without modifying children. But push is simpler.

For `last_le`, using min of node: if min > val, no element <= val. To find last, go right if right.min <= val else left. Need push.

Range add: standard lazy.

Potential recursion depth: segment tree depth ~19, fine. But recursive functions called many times; Python recursion overhead okay. Need set recursionlimit maybe > 1e6 though depth small.

Let's write class or closures for speed.

Implementation details:

```
import sys
def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    intervals = [tuple(map(int, input().split())) for _ in range(N)]
    Q = int(input())
    queries = [int(input()) for _ in range(Q)]
    # sort queries
    order = sorted(range(Q), key=lambda i: queries[i])
    arr = [queries[i] for i in order]
    # rank of original index
    rank = [0]*Q
    for pos, orig in enumerate(order):
        rank[orig] = pos
    # build segtree over arr
    ...
    for L,R in intervals:
        l = first_ge(L)
        r = last_le(R)
        if l <= r:
            range_add(l,r,1)
    # get final arr
    final = [0]*Q
    collect(1,0,Q-1,final)
    out = [str(final[rank[i]]) for i in range(Q)]
    print('\n'.join(out))
```

But note if Q=0? Constraints Q>=1.

Build segment tree recursively:

```
size = 4*Q
mn = [0]*size
mx = [0]*size
lazy = [0]*size

def build(node,l,r):
    if l==r:
        mn[node]=mx[node]=arr[l]
    else:
        mid=(l+r)//2
        build(node*2,l,mid)
        build(node*2+1,mid+1,r)
        mn[node]=mn[node*2] if mn[node*2] < mn[node*2+1] else mn[node*2+1]
        mx[node]=mx[node*2] if mx[node*2] > mx[node*2+1] else mx[node*2+1]
```

Since array sorted, mn=arr[l], mx=arr[r] for segment, but with range add, mn/mx maintain. We can build faster by setting mn[node]=arr[l], mx[node]=arr[r]? Actually for a segment representing [l,r], because array sorted, min is arr[l], max is arr[r]. But after range adds, still sorted, so min at leftmost, max at rightmost. We could avoid storing both? We can store only first and last? Wait range add to a contiguous segment may not align with segment tree nodes. For a segment tree node, the subarray is sorted, so min is at its leftmost index, max at rightmost. We could store `first` and `last` values for each node instead of min/max. Range add adds to all elements in node, so first/last += delta. This is equivalent. For search:
- first_ge: if node.last < val, no element >= val in this segment? Since max is last. Yes. To find first, if left.last >= val go left else right.
- last_le: if node.first > val, no element <= val. Since min is first. To find last, if right.first <= val go right else left.

Range_add:
```
def range_add(node,l,r,ql,qr,val):
    if ql <= l and r <= qr:
        first[node] += val; last[node] += val; lazy[node] += val; return
    push(node)
    mid=(l+r)//2
    if ql <= mid: range_add(left,l,mid,ql,qr,val)
    if qr > mid: range_add(right,mid+1,r,ql,qr,val)
    first[node] = first[left]
    last[node] = last[right]
```

Need ensure `first` and `last` names not conflict with builtins? Fine.

```
def first_ge(node, l, r, val):
    if last[node] < val:
        return Q
    while l < r:
        push(node)
        mid = (l+r)//2
        if last[node*2] >= val:
            node = node*2; r = mid
        else:
            node = node*2+1; l = mid+1
    return l
```

Let's write range_add as recursive with inline push:

```
def range_add(node, l, r, ql, qr, val):
    if ql <= l and r <= qr:
        first[node] += val
        last[node] += val
        lazy[node] += val
        return
    z = lazy[node]
    if z:
        left = node << 1
        right = left | 1
        first[left] += z; last[left] += z; lazy[left] += z
        first[right] += z; last[right] += z; lazy[right] += z
        lazy[node] = 0
    mid = (l+r) >> 1
    if ql <= mid:
        range_add(left, l, mid, ql, qr, val)
    if qr > mid:
        range_add(right, mid+1, r, ql, qr, val)
    first[node] = first[left]
    last[node] = last[right]
```

Need ensure `left` and `right` variables defined before use in pull. If ql<=mid, left child visited; if qr>mid, right visited. If only one side recursed, the other child's values unchanged; pull with first[left], last[right] still correct. Good.

Search iterative with inline push:

```
def first_ge(val):
    if last[1] < val:
        return Q
    node = 1
    l = 0
    r = Q - 1
    while l < r:
        z = lazy[node]
        if z:
            left = node << 1
            right = left | 1
            first[left] += z; last[left] += z; lazy[left] += z
            first[right] += z; last[right] += z; lazy[right] += z
            lazy[node] = 0
        mid = (l+r) >> 1
        left = node << 1
        if last[left] >= val:
            node = left
            r = mid
        else:
            node = left | 1
            l = mid + 1
    return l
```

Similarly last_le.

Need be careful: In `first_ge`, after push, `last[left]` updated. Good. In `last_le`, after push, `first[right]` updated.

Range_add recursive uses `left` and `right` variables. But in Python, assigning `left` inside if and using after is okay if defined. We define `left = node << 1; right = left | 1` before push. Then after recursion, use same. Good.

Build recursive:

```
def build(node, l, r):
    first[node] = arr[l]
    last[node] = arr[r]
    if l < r:
        mid = (l+r)>>1
        left = node<<1
        build(left, l, mid)
        build(left|1, mid+1, r)
```

Because arr sorted, first=arr[l], last=arr[r]. Is this always true for internal node after build? Yes, the segment [l,r] in sorted arr has min arr[l], max arr[r]. No need to set from children. But if we don't set children before parent? We set parent before recursing; children will be set. Fine. For leaves, l==r, first=last=arr[l]. Good.

However, if we set parent first/last from arr, and later range_add partial pulls from children, children must have correct values. Build ensures children correct. Good.

Potential issue: If Q=0? Not possible. If Q=1, build root l=r=0. Search loops while l<r false. Range_add full cover works. `first[1]`, `last[1]`.

Now, after processing all intervals, need final ratings. We can collect by traversing tree and pushing lazy to leaves. Since we only need values at positions, we can do a recursive `collect`:

```
def collect(node, l, r, res):
    if l == r:
        res[l] = first[node]
        return
    z = lazy[node]
    if z:
        left = node<<1; right=left|1
        first[left]+=z; last[left]+=z; lazy[left]+=z
        first[right]+=z; last[right]+=z; lazy[right]+=z
        lazy[node]=0
    mid=...
    collect(left,l,mid,res)
    collect(right,mid+1,r,res)
```

This visits all nodes O(Q). Or we can point query each original rank using iterative descent, but collect is O(Q) and simple. Recursion over ~2Q nodes, depth 19, okay. But function calls ~2Q=6e5, fine. We can also collect iteratively with stack to avoid recursion? Not necessary.

Actually, after range_add operations, some lazy values remain at internal nodes. `first[node]` for a node includes all lazy applied to that node, but leaves may not. Collect pushes to leaves. Good.

Then answer:
```
res = [0]*Q
collect(1,0,Q-1,res)
out = [str(res[rank[i]]) for i in range(Q)]
sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
```

Need ensure `rank` maps original query index to position. We sorted `order = sorted(range(Q), key=lambda i: queries[i])`. If duplicate X, sorted by original index as tie? `sorted(range(Q), key=lambda i: queries[i])` is stable, so duplicates keep original order. Does order among equal initial ratings matter? No, equal ratings indistinguishable. Range add will treat them as contiguous. If duplicates, sorted order arbitrary but stable. At end, rank maps correctly. Good.

Let's test sample1 with code mentally. Already did.

Now, let's consider if the operation should apply to rating between L_i and R_i inclusive before contest. Our range add uses current ratings. Good.

Let's think about maximum rating: initial max 5e5, N 2e5, final max 7e5. Segment tree values fit int.

Now, is there any hidden issue with using `first` and `last` instead of min/max if the array is sorted but after range add to a segment that is not aligned with segment tree node, does each node's subarray remain sorted? Yes, global array sorted, so any subarray sorted. Range add adds same value to a contiguous subarray, preserving order within and with outside as proven. So node first/last min/max.

Let's prove global sorted after range add with duplicates more formally:
Let a[0..n-1] nondecreasing. Let p = min{i | a[i] >= L}, q = max{i | a[i] <= R}. If p>q, no change. Else for i<p, a[i] < L, so a[i] <= L-1. For p<=i<=q, a[i] ∈ [L,R]. For i>q, a[i] > R, so a[i] >= R+1.
After adding 1 to p..q:
- For i=p-1 (if exists): a[p-1] <= L-1 < L+1 <= a[p]+1.
- For i=q: a[q]+1 <= R+1 <= a[q+1] (if q+1 exists, since a[q+1] >= R+1).
Thus nondecreasing. If p=0 or q=n-1 boundaries okay. Good.

Now, search functions using first/last:
`first_ge(val)` returns p. Since array sorted, p is first index with a[p]>=val. Our descent:
At node [l,r], if last[node] < val, no element >=val in this segment. At root if none return Q. Else if left.last >= val, first is in left; else left all < val, first in right. This is correct.
`last_le(val)` returns q. If first[node] > val, no element <=val. At root if none return -1. Else if right.first <= val, last is in right; else right all > val, last in left. Correct.

Need handle `val` maybe > max or < min. If L > current max, first_ge returns Q, last_le maybe some index, l>r skip. If R < current min, last_le returns -1, skip.

Now, let's think about performance of `range_add` recursive. We can maybe implement range_add iteratively with a stack to avoid recursion overhead? Let's consider.

Recursive range_add:
```
def range_add(node,l,r,ql,qr,val):
    if ql <= l and r <= qr: ...
    push...
    mid...
    if ql <= mid: range_add(left,...)
    if qr > mid: range_add(right,...)
    pull...
```
This is standard. Number of recursive calls per range add is O(log n) for full cover? Actually for a range, it visits O(log n) nodes that are fully covered plus O(log n) boundary nodes. The number of function calls is about 2*log n to 4*log n. For n=3e5, ~80 calls. N=2e5 => 16M calls. Could be okay but maybe high. We can optimize by using an iterative segment tree with lazy? Let's see if we can implement range add iteratively using the "segment tree with lazy propagation" from AtCoder Library.

AtCoder lazy segtree iterative:
- size power of two, log.
- `all_apply(k, f)` updates node k and stores lazy.
- `push(k)` propagates lazy to children.
- `range_apply(l,r,f)`:
```
l += size; r += size
for i in range(log,0,-1):
    if ((l>>i)<<i) != l: push(l>>i)
    if ((r>>i)<<i) != r: push((r-1)>>i)
l2=l; r2=r
while l<r:
    if l&1: all_apply(l,f); l+=1
    if r&1: r-=1; all_apply(r,f)
    l>>=1; r>>=1
l=l2; r=r2
for i in range(1,log+1):
    if ((l>>i)<<i) != l: update(l>>i)
    if ((r>>i)<<i) != r: update((r-1)>>i)
```
This is more complex but avoids recursion and is fast. We can adapt with first/last arrays of length 2*size. Need handle padding. But we can set padding values appropriately and ensure range_apply only real range. Search first_ge/last_le can also be iterative using the tree. This might be more efficient and memory similar (2*size ~1,048,576). Let's explore implementing this to produce robust final code.

Iterative lazy segment tree with first/last:

Let `n = Q`, `size = 1 << (n-1).bit_length()`, `log = size.bit_length() - 1`.

Arrays:
`first = [0]*(2*size)`, `last = [0]*(2*size)`, `lazy = [0]*(2*size)`.

Initialize leaves:
For i in 0..n-1: first[size+i] = last[size+i] = arr[i].
For i in n..size-1: padding. Need set first and last to something. For range_apply, we won't apply to padding. For search, we need padding not interfere. We can set padding first=last = INF (10**18). But if we set INF, then for a node covering real and padding, `last[node]` will be INF, `first[node]` real first. This breaks the property that `last[node]` is max of real segment? It is max including padding, which is INF. For `first_ge`, if val <= INF, it might descend into padding when no real element >= val. We can check if result >= n then return n. But also, for a node with real and padding, `last[node]` = INF, so `first_ge` may think there is an element >= val and descend. It will eventually return a padding index if no real >= val. We can handle by if pos >= n: return n. But what if there is a real element >= val and also padding? It will find the first real because real values come before padding and are <= INF. Since padding is after real, first_ge will descend left as long as left.last >= val. For a node containing real and padding, left child may be real, right child padding. If left.last >= val, go left; else go right (padding). If no real >=val, returns first padding. So result >=n. Good.

For `last_le(val)`, padding INF > val, so it will never choose padding if val < INF. But what about a node containing real and padding: first is real first. If right child is padding with first=INF > val, it goes left. So it will find last real <=val. Good.

Range_apply [l,r) with l<r<n. Standard iterative range apply may update nodes that include padding if r==n? We call with r exclusive = q+1 where q < n, so r <= n. If r == n, the iterative algorithm may update nodes that cover exactly [some, size) including padding? Let's check standard range_apply uses half-open [l,r) in leaf coordinates. If r = n (not size), it will not include leaves beyond n because r is n, not size. It decomposes [l,n) into nodes that exactly cover real leaves only? In a segment tree with size power of two, any node corresponds to a contiguous interval of leaves. If r=n, the decomposition of [l,n) will not include any node extending beyond n? Actually if n is not power of two, the interval [l,n) may be decomposed into nodes that are fully contained in [l,n). Since n is the boundary, no node extends beyond n because that would include leaves >=n, not in interval. The iterative algorithm uses r as leaf index, so it only selects nodes whose segment is within [l,r). Thus no padding. Good.

But build internal nodes: We need compute first/last for all nodes. If padding INF, for a node with both real and padding, first = left.first (real), last = right.last (INF). For a node entirely padding, first=last=INF. For a node entirely real, first=left.first, last=right.last. We can build:
```
for i in range(size-1,0,-1):
    first[i] = first[i<<1]
    last[i] = last[i<<1|1]
```
Since leaves sorted with padding INF at end, this gives first = leftmost leaf value, last = rightmost leaf value. For a node entirely real, last is rightmost real. For mixed, last INF. Good.

Range add full node: `first[k] += val; last[k] += val; lazy[k] += val`. But if node contains padding INF and we accidentally apply to it, INF+val remains huge; but we won't apply to padding. If a node is mixed real/padding, it will never be fully covered by a range [l,r) with r<=n because the node extends beyond n. So no full apply to mixed node. Partial updates will descend to real nodes only. Good.

Search first_ge with iterative lazy tree:
We need a function to push ancestors of a leaf? For range_apply, we need push along paths before applying to ensure children values up-to-date. For search, we can descend from root, pushing lazy at each node. Since first/last at root include lazy, children may not. We can implement:

```
def push(k):
    z = lazy[k]
    if z:
        left = k<<1; right=left|1
        first[left] += z; last[left] += z; lazy[left] += z
        first[right] += z; last[right] += z; lazy[right] += z
        lazy[k] = 0
```

`first_ge(val)`:
```
def first_ge(val):
    if last[1] < val:
        return n
    k=1
    while k < size:
        push(k)
        left=k<<1
        if last[left] >= val:
            k=left
        else:
            k=left|1
    pos = k - size
    return pos if pos < n else n
```
This descends to leaf. If leaf is padding, pos>=n. Good.

`last_le(val)`:
```
def last_le(val):
    if first[1] > val:
        return -1
    k=1
    while k < size:
        push(k)
        right=k<<1|1
        if first[right] <= val:
            k=right
        else:
            k=left? k<<1
    pos = k - size
    return pos if pos < n else -1? 
```
If it descends to padding? For last_le, if first[1] <= val, and val < INF, it should never choose padding because padding first=INF > val. So pos < n. But to be safe, if pos>=n return n-1? Actually if all real > val, root.first would be > val? If some real <=val, first[1] <= val, so there is a real <=val. The descent will find the last real <=val, not padding. So pos<n. Good.

Range_apply iterative with lazy:

We'll implement standard AtCoder style. Need update first/last for ancestors after applying.

Pseudo:

```
def all_apply(k, f):
    first[k] += f
    last[k] += f
    if k < size:
        lazy[k] += f

def push(k):
    z = lazy[k]
    if z:
        all_apply(k<<1, z)
        all_apply(k<<1|1, z)
        lazy[k] = 0

def update(k):
    first[k] = first[k<<1]
    last[k] = last[k<<1|1]
```

Range apply [l,r) 0-index half-open:
```
def range_add(l, r, val):
    if l >= r: return
    l += size
    r += size
    l0, r0 = l, r
    for i in range(log, 0, -1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
            push(l0 >> i)
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
            push((r0 - 1) >> i)
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            all_apply(l, val)
            l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1
            all_apply(r, val)
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1
    l, r = l0, r0
    for i in range(1, log+1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
            update(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
            update((r - 1) >> i)
```

This is standard. Need ensure `all_apply` on leaf (k>=size) doesn't update lazy. In our arrays length 2*size, lazy for leaves can be ignored. In `all_apply`, if k < size: lazy[k] += f. But if k is leaf, lazy not used. We can just always `lazy[k] += f`? For leaves, lazy array exists but push never called on leaf because k<size check in push? In range_apply, all_apply may be called on leaf nodes. If we update lazy[leaf], it's harmless but memory. However, in `push(k)`, k is internal (<size). It calls all_apply on children, which may be leaves; if all_apply updates lazy for leaves, okay. But later if we push a leaf? We never call push on leaf because in range_apply push loop uses k>>i where k is leaf+size? Let's check: l0>>i could be 1? Actually if i=log, l0>>i >=1. It can be a leaf? For i=1, l0>>1 could be a parent of leaves, internal. So push only internal. Search descends while k<size, so push internal. Thus lazy on leaves unused. We can still update for simplicity. But if all_apply on leaf updates lazy, and later update(k) uses first/last of children, lazy leaf ignored. Fine.

Need be careful with `push` in range_apply before applying: The standard conditions push ancestors of l and r-1 to ensure that when we later all_apply to a node, its children are up-to-date? Actually all_apply to a node doesn't require children up-to-date because it just updates node's first/last and lazy. But before partial updates, we need push along boundary paths so that when we descend? The iterative range_apply doesn't descend; it applies to nodes directly. The push before is needed to ensure that if we later update ancestors using children values, the children values are correct? Let's recall standard lazy segtree: Before applying to range, push ancestors of l and r-1 so that any pending lazy on nodes that will be partially covered is propagated to children, because we will later update those children? Actually range_apply applies to nodes that are fully covered; if a node has pending lazy and we all_apply to it, that's fine. But if a node is an ancestor of a boundary and will be updated after, we need its children to reflect all lazy before recomputing. The push loop handles that. We'll trust standard.

However, our `update(k)` sets first[k] = first[left], last[k] = last[right]. If children have pending lazy, their first/last include it. Good.

Let's test iterative range_apply with small example manually? Might be okay. But implementing standard lazy correctly is error-prone. Recursive is simpler and less likely to have bugs. Given final answer should be correct, maybe recursive is safer. Time? Let's estimate with recursive and iterative searches. N=2e5, Q=3e5. Recursive range_add calls ~ maybe 4log=80 per contest =16M. Python 16M recursive calls might be okay but could be near limit. Iterative range_apply uses loops over log for push and update: ~2*log + log = ~57 iterations per range_add, no recursion. That is faster. Searches iterative ~19 each. Total per contest ~57 + 38 =95 loop iterations, 19M simple operations. Recursive range_add 16M function calls might be slower than iterative 11M loops. Maybe we should implement iterative lazy segment tree for performance. But we need be confident.

Let's thoroughly verify iterative lazy segment tree with first/last and padding.

First, build:
```
size = 1
log = 0
while size < n:
    size <<= 1
    log += 1
first = [0]*(2*size)
last = [0]*(2*size)
lazy = [0]*(2*size)

    first[size+i] = last[size+i] = arr[i]

    first[size+i] = last[size+i] = INF
for i in range(size-1, 0, -1):
    first[i] = first[i<<1]
    last[i] = last[i<<1|1]
```
Because leaves sorted with padding INF, first of node = leftmost leaf, last = rightmost leaf. For a node entirely real, first=arr[l], last=arr[r]. For mixed, last=INF. Good.

`all_apply(k, f)`:
```
first[k] += f

    lazy[k] += f
```
If k is a node containing padding and we accidentally apply, first/last become INF+f. But we won't. If k is a real node, okay. If k is a mixed node? `push(k)`:
```

    all_apply(k<<1, z)
    all_apply(k<<1|1, z)

```
If k is internal but one child is padding, all_apply adds to padding first/last. That's okay; padding values become INF+z, still huge. But if later we build? We never rebuild. Search last_le with val<=7e5 ignores INF+z. first_ge may return padding if no real; pos>=n. Good. However, if padding values become INF+z and z accumulates many times, still > val. Fine.

But wait: If a mixed node has lazy? Could a mixed node ever get lazy? We never full-apply to mixed node, so no. But a mixed node could be an ancestor of a real node that got lazy? Lazy is stored only on nodes that are fully applied. If a mixed node is never fully applied, lazy[mixed]=0. Its children may have lazy. `push` on an ancestor of mixed? In range_apply, push loop may call push on ancestors of l/r. If an ancestor has lazy, it propagates to children, possibly including mixed node. Then mixed node's lazy becomes z (because all_apply on child). Wait, if an ancestor that is fully real? Let's consider root may be mixed if n not power of two. Can root get lazy? Range_apply [0,n) does not cover root because root segment [0,size) includes padding, so root is not fully covered. Thus root never gets lazy. But an ancestor of a real range that is fully real could get lazy. Mixed nodes are those whose segment crosses n. They are never fully covered, so they never get lazy from range_apply directly. But they can receive lazy from push of their parent? If parent is fully real and mixed child? A parent of a mixed node is also mixed (because it contains padding). So no fully real parent has mixed child. Thus mixed nodes never receive lazy? Wait if root mixed, push root? root lazy=0. If a node is mixed, all ancestors are mixed. Since mixed nodes never get lazy, they won't push lazy to children. So padding values remain INF. Good. But even if they did, okay.

Now, `range_add(l, r, val)` with l,r 0-index inclusive? We'll use half-open [l, r+1). Let's define function `range_add(l, r, val)` where l and r are inclusive indices. It calls `_range_add(l, r+1, val)`.

Standard iterative range_apply:
```
def range_add(l, r, val):  # inclusive
    if l > r: return
    l += size
    r += size + 1  # exclusive
    l0, r0 = l, r
    for i in range(log, 0, -1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
            push(l0 >> i)
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
            push((r0 - 1) >> i)
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            all_apply(l, val)
            l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1
            all_apply(r, val)
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1
    l, r = l0, r0
    for i in range(1, log+1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
            update(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
            update((r - 1) >> i)
```
Need understand conditions. In AtCoder Library, for range_apply(l,r) half-open:
```
l += size; r += size
for i in range(log,0,-1):
    if ((l >> i) << i) != l: push(l >> i)
    if ((r >> i) << i) != r: push((r-1) >> i)
...
for i in range(1,log+1):
    if ((l >> i) << i) != l: update(l >> i)
    if ((r >> i) << i) != r: update((r-1) >> i)
```
Here after l,r shifted, they are leaf indices. The condition checks if l is not aligned to a segment of size 2^i. If not aligned, then the ancestor at level i may have been partially modified and needs push/update. This is standard. We'll use exactly.

Now, search functions with iterative tree and lazy:
`first_ge(val)`:
```
def first_ge(val):
    if last[1] < val:
        return n
    k = 1
    while k < size:
        z = lazy[k]
        if z:
            all_apply(k<<1, z)
            all_apply(k<<1|1, z)

        left = k<<1
        if last[left] >= val:
            k = left
        else:

```
Need if k goes to padding, pos>=n. Good. But if k is padding leaf, its last=INF>=val, so it can be chosen. Return n.

`last_le(val)`:
```
def last_le(val):
    if first[1] > val:
        return -1
    k = 1
    while k < size:
        z = lazy[k]
        if z:
            all_apply(k<<1, z)

        if first[right] <= val:
            k = right

```
If val < INF, it won't pick padding because first[padding]=INF > val. But what if padding first got INF+z, still > val. So pos<n. Return pos. If somehow pos>=n, return -1? But if root.first<=val, there is real <=val, so not. We can assert or just return pos if pos<n else -1. For safety:

```
But if pos>=n, that would be wrong? It shouldn't happen. Use `return pos` maybe. But to avoid out-of-range, if pos>=n return n-1? No, if no real <=val, root.first would be INF? Actually if all real > val, root.first = first real > val, so root.first > val and we return -1. So pos<n. Good.

Now, range_add inclusive:
```
def range_add(l, r, val):
    if l > r:
        return
    l += size
    r += size + 1
    l0, r0 = l, r
    for i in range(log, 0, -1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
            push(l0 >> i)
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
            push((r0 - 1) >> i)
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            all_apply(l, val)
            l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1
            all_apply(r, val)
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1
    l, r = l0, r0
    for i in range(1, log+1):
        if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
            update(l >> i)
        if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
            update((r - 1) >> i)
```

Need define `push(k)` and `all_apply(k, f)` as nested functions. Function calls inside loops: range_add calls push/update/all_apply many times. That's okay but maybe overhead. We can inline all_apply in push? But standard functions fine. Number of operations ~N*(log push + log update + log apply) ~2e5*57 =11.4M function calls to push/update/all_apply? Actually each push is a function call, each all_apply is a function call. In range_apply, push loop calls push up to 2log times, update loop up to 2log, apply loop up to 2log all_apply. Total ~6log=114 function calls per contest =>22.8M. Similar to recursive. But many are simple. Could inline to improve. But code complexity.

Maybe recursive with inline push is simpler and not much slower. Let's compare:
- Recursive range_add: ~80 function calls to range_add itself, plus inline push no function calls. Searches iterative with inline push. Total ~80+38=118 "operations" per contest, but function calls only range_add. 16M range_add calls. Iterative standard: ~114 function calls to push/update/all_apply. Similar. Recursive might be okay.

Let's choose recursive for correctness, but optimize searches iterative and range_add recursive. We can also implement range_add iteratively using an explicit stack to avoid recursion? Let's implement iterative lazy segment tree carefully and test with sample.

Let's write code in final with iterative lazy.

Detailed implementation plan:

```
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    intervals = [tuple(map(int, input().split())) for _ in range(N)]
    Q = int(input())
    queries = [int(input()) for _ in range(Q)]

    order = sorted(range(Q), key=lambda i: queries[i])
    arr = [queries[i] for i in order]
    rank = [0]*Q
    for pos, idx in enumerate(order):
        rank[idx] = pos

    size = 1
    log = 0
    while size < Q:
        size <<= 1
        log += 1

    INF = 10**18
    first = [0]*(2*size)
    last = [0]*(2*size)
    lazy = [0]*(2*size)

    for i in range(Q):
        first[size+i] = last[size+i] = arr[i]
    for i in range(Q, size):
        first[size+i] = last[size+i] = INF

    for i in range(size-1, 0, -1):
        first[i] = first[i<<1]
        last[i] = last[i<<1|1]

    def all_apply(k, f):
        first[k] += f
        last[k] += f
        if k < size:
            lazy[k] += f

    def push(k):
        z = lazy[k]
        if z:
            all_apply(k<<1, z)
            all_apply(k<<1|1, z)
            lazy[k] = 0

    def update(k):
        first[k] = first[k<<1]
        last[k] = last[k<<1|1]

    def range_add(l, r, f):  # inclusive
        if l > r:
            return
        l += size
        r += size + 1
        l0, r0 = l, r
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                push(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                push((r0 - 1) >> i)
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                all_apply(l, f)
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                all_apply(r, f)
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        l, r = l0, r0
        for i in range(1, log+1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                update(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                update((r - 1) >> i)

    def first_ge(val):
        if last[1] < val:
            return Q
        k = 1
        while k < size:
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                all_apply(k<<1, z)
                all_apply(k<<1|1, z)
                lazy[k] = 0
            left = k << 1
            if last[left] >= val:
                k = left
            else:
                k = left | 1
        pos = k - size
        return pos if pos < Q else Q

    def last_le(val):
        if first[1] > val:
            return -1
        k = 1
        while k < size:
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                all_apply(k<<1, z)
                all_apply(k<<1|1, z)
                lazy[k] = 0
            right = (k << 1) | 1
            if first[right] <= val:
                k = right
            else:
                k = k << 1
        pos = k - size
        return pos if pos < Q else -1  # should not happen

    for L, R in intervals:
        l = first_ge(L)
        r = last_le(R)
        if l <= r:
            range_add(l, r, 1)

    # collect final values
    res = [0]*Q
    # We can get leaf values by pushing all lazy. Since we need only Q leaves.
    # Iterative collect: for i in range(size): push? We can do DFS.
    # Simpler: use a stack to traverse and push lazy.
    stack = [1]
    while stack:
        k = stack.pop()
        if k >= size:
            pos = k - size
            if pos < Q:
                res[pos] = first[k]
        else:
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                all_apply(k<<1, z)
                all_apply(k<<1|1, z)
                lazy[k] = 0
            stack.append(k<<1)
            stack.append(k<<1|1)
    out = [str(res[rank[i]]) for i in range(Q)]
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))
```

Let's implement parsing with data list:

```
import sys
def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    idx = 0
    N = data[idx]; idx += 1
    Ls = [0]*N
    Rs = [0]*N
    for i in range(N):
        Ls[i] = data[idx]; Rs[i] = data[idx+1]; idx += 2
    Q = data[idx]; idx += 1
    queries = data[idx:idx+Q]
    # idx += Q
```
This stores Ls and Rs lists of ints (references to int objects from data). If we delete data, ints still referenced by Ls/Rs/queries. Good. Using two lists avoids tuple overhead. Let's do this.

Parsing:
```

N = int(input())
Ls = [0]*N
Rs = [0]*N
for i in range(N):

Then:
```
order = sorted(range(Q), key=lambda i: queries[i])
rank = [0]*Q
arr = [0]*Q
for pos, idx in enumerate(order):
    rank[idx] = pos
    arr[pos] = queries[idx]

```

Now, segment tree build with arr:
```
size = 1
log = 0
while size < Q:
    size <<= 1
    log += 1
first = [0]*(2*size)
last = [0]*(2*size)
lazy = [0]*(2*size)
INF = 10**9  # or 10**18 if list
for i in range(Q):
    first[size+i] = last[size+i] = arr[i]
for i in range(Q, size):
    first[size+i] = last[size+i] = INF
for i in range(size-1, 0, -1):
    first[i] = first[i<<1]
    last[i] = last[i<<1|1]
```

Let's think about a potential bug in `range_add` update loop: We use `l, r = l0, r0` after apply. Then for i in range(1, log+1): if ((l >> i) << i) != l: update(l >> i); if ((r >> i) << i) != r: update((r - 1) >> i). Here `r` is exclusive leaf index. If r0 == size? For range [0,Q-1] with Q=size, r0=2*size. Then `(r0 >> i) << i` equals r0 for all i? r0 is power of two, so aligned, no update. But root was updated by all_apply, no need update. If range [0,Q-1] with Q not power of two, r0=Q+size < 2*size, not aligned, update ancestors. Good.

Let's verify with range_add [0,0] Q=3, l0=4,r0=5. Update loop i=1: r0=5, ((5>>1)<<1)=4 !=5 -> update((5-1)>>1=2). i=2: r0=5, ((5>>2)<<2)=4 !=5 -> update(1). Good. It updates node2 and root. What about l0=4 aligned, no update on l side. Good.

Now, one subtlety: In `range_add`, the push loop should push ancestors of l and r-1 before applying. We use `if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0: push(l0 >> i)`. For l0=4, i=1: ((4>>1)<<1)=4 ==4, no push. But node2 (l0>>1=2) might have lazy from previous operations. Do we need to push node2 before applying to a range that starts at l=0? In our example, node2 had lazy=1 before range_add [0,0]. The push loop with l0=4 did NOT push node2 for i=1 because l0 is aligned to 2. But r0=5 not aligned, so it pushed node2 via r side: `if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0: push((r0-1)>>i)` -> push(2). So node2 was pushed. Good. What if range_add [0,1] (l0=4,r0=6)? l0 aligned, r0=6: i=1, ((6>>1)<<1)=6 ==6, no push; i=2, ((6>>2)<<2)=4 !=6 -> push((6-1)>>2=1) push root. It does not push node2. But range [0,1] fully covers node2, so we will all_apply node2 directly. If node2 had lazy, all_apply node2 adds to its first/last/lazy; no need to push to children because we're applying to the whole node. Later update ancestors uses node2.first/last which include lazy. So no need to push node2. Good.

What if range_add [1,1] (l0=5,r0=6)? l0=5 not aligned at i=1 -> push(l0>>1=2) pushes node2. r0=6 aligned? i=1 aligned, i=2 not aligned -> push(1). Good. So boundary nodes pushed. Standard.

Now, search functions push along path. If a node has lazy and we search, we push it. This is necessary because we need children values to decide descent. Good.

Now, collect: We traverse all nodes and push lazy. But if we use stack and push lazy, we don't update internal nodes; not needed. We append children. For leaves, res. Good.

Let's think about if `first_ge` and `last_le` are called after some range_add left lazy on nodes. They push along the path, which modifies the tree. This is okay. But pushing in search can increase the number of nonzero lazy entries? It moves lazy down. It doesn't change values. It may cause `first` and `last` of ancestors to remain correct. Good.

Now, let's consider if `first_ge` and `last_le` should use `last[left]` after pushing, but what if `left` is beyond array length? In a complete binary tree of size `size`, children exist for k<size. We only descend while k<size. Good.

Let's write final code with iterative lazy segment tree. Need ensure no syntax errors.

Let's draft final code:

```
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    Ls = [0]*N
    Rs = [0]*N
    for i in range(N):
        L, R = map(int, input().split())
        Ls[i] = L
        Rs[i] = R

    Q = int(input())
    queries = [0]*Q
    for i in range(Q):
        queries[i] = int(input())

    order = sorted(range(Q), key=queries.__getitem__)
    rank = [0]*Q
    arr = [0]*Q
    for pos, idx in enumerate(order):
        rank[idx] = pos
        arr[pos] = queries[idx]
    del queries, order

    size = 1
    log = 0
    while size < Q:
        size <<= 1
        log += 1

    INF = 10**18
    first = [0] * (2 * size)
    last = [0] * (2 * size)
    lazy = [0] * (2 * size)

    for i in range(Q):
        first[size + i] = arr[i]
        last[size + i] = arr[i]
    for i in range(Q, size):
        first[size + i] = INF
        last[size + i] = INF

    for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        first[i] = first[i << 1]
        last[i] = last[i << 1 | 1]

    def all_apply(k, f):
        first[k] += f
        last[k] += f
        if k < size:
            lazy[k] += f

    def push(k):
        z = lazy[k]
        if z:
            all_apply(k << 1, z)
            all_apply(k << 1 | 1, z)
            lazy[k] = 0

    def update(k):
        first[k] = first[k << 1]
        last[k] = last[k << 1 | 1]

    def range_add(l, r, f):
        if l > r:
            return
        l += size
        r += size + 1
        l0 = l
        r0 = r
        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                push(l0 >> i)
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                push((r0 - 1) >> i)
        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                all_apply(l, f)
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                all_apply(r, f)
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1
        l = l0
        r = r0
        for i in range(1, log + 1):
            if ((l >> i) << i) != l:
                update(l >> i)
            if ((r >> i) << i) != r:
                update((r - 1) >> i)

    def first_ge(val):
        if last[1] < val:
            return Q
        k = 1
        while k < size:
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                all_apply(k << 1, z)
                all_apply(k << 1 | 1, z)
                lazy[k] = 0
            left = k << 1
            if last[left] >= val:
                k = left
            else:
                k = left | 1
        pos = k - size
        return pos if pos < Q else Q

    def last_le(val):
        if first[1] > val:
            return -1
        k = 1
        while k < size:
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                all_apply(k << 1, z)
                all_apply(k << 1 | 1, z)
                lazy[k] = 0
            right = (k << 1) | 1
            if first[right] <= val:
                k = right
            else:
                k = k << 1
        pos = k - size
        return pos if pos < Q else -1

    for L, R in zip(Ls, Rs):
        l = first_ge(L)
        r = last_le(R)
        if l <= r:
            range_add(l, r, 1)

    res = [0] * Q
    stack = [1]
    while stack:
        k = stack.pop()
        if k >= size:
            pos = k - size
            if pos < Q:
                res[pos] = first[k]
        else:
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                all_apply(k << 1, z)
                all_apply(k << 1 | 1, z)
                lazy[k] = 0
            stack.append(k << 1)
            stack.append(k << 1 | 1)

    out = [str(res[rank[i]]) for i in range(Q)]
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Need check `first[i] = first[i << 1 | 1]` precedence: In Python, `<<` has lower precedence than `|`? Actually shift has higher precedence than bitwise OR? Let's recall precedence: shifts have higher precedence than bitwise AND, XOR, OR. So `i << 1 | 1` is `(i << 1) | 1`. Good. But to be safe, use `(i << 1) | 1` in code. In `last[i] = last[i << 1 | 1]`, same. We'll use parentheses for clarity.

In `all_apply(k << 1 | 1, z)`, precedence okay but use `(k << 1) | 1`.

Now, let's think about `range_add` update loop: We set `l = l0; r = r0` after apply. But `l0` and `r0` are leaf indices. In the update loop, the conditions use `l` and `r` as original shifted values. Standard. Good.

Potential bug: In `range_add`, after apply loop, `l` and `r` have been shifted and mutated. We restore. Good.

Potential bug: In `range_add`, if `log` is 0, `range(log,0,-1)` and `range(1,log+1)` empty. Good.

Potential bug: In `first_ge`, if `last[1] < val`, return Q. But if Q=0? no. If Q=1, size=1, `last[1]` exists. Good.

Potential bug: In `last_le`, if `first[1] > val`, return -1. If Q=1, size=1, while k<size false, pos=0. Good.

Potential bug: In collect, if size=1, stack [1], k>=size true, pos=0, res[0]=first[1]. Good.

Now, let's consider if `range_add` can be called with l=0,r=Q-1 when Q=size (power of two). Then l0=size, r0=2*size. Push loop: l0 aligned, r0 aligned, no push. Apply loop: l=size,r=2*size. while: l=size/2, r=size; eventually l=1,r=2? Let's simulate size=4, l=4,r=8:
l=4,r=8 -> l=2,r=4 -> l=1,r=2. l&1 -> all_apply(1), l=2. r=2. l=1,r=1 end. So root applied. Update loop: l0=4,r0=8. For i=1: l aligned? ((4>>1)<<1)=4 ==4; r aligned? ((8>>1)<<1)=8 ==8. i=2 aligned. No update. Root lazy set. Good.

Now, if Q=3, range_add [0,2] applied node2 and leaf6, update node3 and root. We tested.

Now, let's think about if `first_ge` and `last_le` push lazy along path, and then `range_add` update loop updates ancestors. If search pushed lazy from an ancestor, the ancestor's first/last remain correct. But the ancestor's lazy is cleared. Later range_add update loop might update that ancestor based on children. Children now have correct values. Good.

Now, let's consider if search functions should update ancestors after pushing? Pushing doesn't change the node's own first/last, so no update needed. But it changes children; the parent's first/last still equal left.first/right.last? Let's verify: Before push, parent.first = old left.first + z, parent.last = old right.last + z. After push, left.first = old left.first + z, right.last = old right.last + z. So parent.first == left.first, parent.last == right.last. Thus no update needed. Good.

Now, let's think about if `all_apply` on a node with padding INF and then `update` parent could set parent.last to INF+f. If a mixed node somehow gets lazy, its first/last become real+f and INF+f. If it did, first/last INF+f. Search first_ge might return padding pos>=Q. Fine. But if padding node gets lazy, and later range_add update parent uses last=INF+f, still >val. No issue. Recursive version:

```
def range_add(node, nl, nr, ql, qr, val):
    if ql <= nl and nr <= qr:
        first[node] += val; last[node] += val; lazy[node] += val; return
    z = lazy[node]
    if z:
        left=node<<1; right=left|1
        first[left]+=z; last[left]+=z; lazy[left]+=z
        first[right]+=z; last[right]+=z; lazy[right]+=z
        lazy[node]=0
    mid=(nl+nr)>>1
    if ql <= mid: range_add(left,nl,mid,ql,qr,val)
    if qr > mid: range_add(right,mid+1,nr,ql,qr,val)
    first[node]=first[left]; last[node]=last[right]
```
Search iterative with inline push. This has no update/all_apply helper calls, only range_add recursive calls. Number of range_add calls ~16M. Recursion overhead maybe okay. Code much shorter. Actually PyPy can do ~50M simple ops/sec, but recursive calls maybe 10M/sec. 16M ~1.6s. Plus other loops, maybe 3s. Acceptable? AtCoder time limit unknown. The iterative inline version would be faster but more code. Since final answer should be correct and efficient, either is fine. I lean iterative inline for performance, but risk bugs. Let's write iterative inline `range_add` carefully.

```
def range_add(l, r, f):
    if l > r:
        return
    l += size
    r += size + 1
    l0 = l
    r0 = r

    # push ancestors
    for i in range(log, 0, -1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
            k = l0 >> i
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                left = k << 1
                right = left | 1
                first[left] += z
                last[left] += z
                if left < size:
                    lazy[left] += z
                first[right] += z
                last[right] += z
                if right < size:
                    lazy[right] += z
                lazy[k] = 0
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
            k = (r0 - 1) >> i
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                left = k << 1
                right = left | 1
                first[left] += z
                last[left] += z
                if left < size:
                    lazy[left] += z
                first[right] += z
                last[right] += z
                if right < size:
                    lazy[right] += z
                lazy[k] = 0

    # apply
    while l < r:
        if l & 1:
            first[l] += f
            last[l] += f
            if l < size:
                lazy[l] += f
            l += 1
        if r & 1:
            r -= 1
            first[r] += f
            last[r] += f
            if r < size:
                lazy[r] += f
        l >>= 1
        r >>= 1

    # update ancestors
    for i in range(1, log + 1):
        if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
            k = l0 >> i
            first[k] = first[k << 1]
            last[k] = last[(k << 1) | 1]
        if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
            k = (r0 - 1) >> i
            first[k] = first[k << 1]
            last[k] = last[(k << 1) | 1]
```

Need be careful: In push loop, if l and r conditions refer to same k, we push twice. Second time z=0, harmless. But if first push propagates lazy and clears, second push does nothing. Good.

In update loop, if same k updated twice, second update same values, harmless.

Search functions with inline push:

```
def first_ge(val):
    if last[1] < val:
        return Q
    k = 1
    while k < size:
        z = lazy[k]
        if z:
            left = k << 1
            right = left | 1
            first[left] += z
            last[left] += z
            if left < size:
                lazy[left] += z
            first[right] += z
            last[right] += z
            if right < size:
                lazy[right] += z
            lazy[k] = 0
        left = k << 1
        if last[left] >= val:
            k = left
        else:
            k = left | 1
    pos = k - size
    return pos if pos < Q else Q
```

`last_le` similar.

Let's decide final code version. I think iterative inline is robust and efficient. Let's ensure no syntax errors with parentheses.

Let's write final code carefully:

```
import sys

def main():
    input = sys.stdin.buffer.readline
    N = int(input())
    Ls = [0]*N
    Rs = [0]*N
    for i in range(N):
        L, R = map(int, input().split())
        Ls[i] = L
        Rs[i] = R

    Q = int(input())
    queries = [0]*Q
    for i in range(Q):
        queries[i] = int(input())

    order = sorted(range(Q), key=queries.__getitem__)
    rank = [0]*Q
    arr = [0]*Q
    for pos, idx in enumerate(order):
        rank[idx] = pos
        arr[pos] = queries[idx]
    del queries, order

    size = 1
    log = 0
    while size < Q:
        size <<= 1
        log += 1

    INF = 10**18
    first = [0] * (2 * size)
    last = [0] * (2 * size)
    lazy = [0] * (2 * size)

    for i in range(Q):
        first[size + i] = arr[i]
        last[size + i] = arr[i]
    for i in range(Q, size):
        first[size + i] = INF
        last[size + i] = INF

    for i in range(size - 1, 0, -1):
        first[i] = first[i << 1]
        last[i] = last[(i << 1) | 1]

    del arr

    def first_ge(val):
        if last[1] < val:
            return Q
        k = 1
        while k < size:
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                left = k << 1
                right = left | 1
                first[left] += z
                last[left] += z
                if left < size:
                    lazy[left] += z
                first[right] += z
                last[right] += z
                if right < size:
                    lazy[right] += z
                lazy[k] = 0
            left = k << 1
            if last[left] >= val:
                k = left
            else:
                k = left | 1
        pos = k - size
        return pos if pos < Q else Q

    def last_le(val):
        if first[1] > val:
            return -1
        k = 1
        while k < size:
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                left = k << 1
                right = left | 1
                first[left] += z
                last[left] += z
                if left < size:
                    lazy[left] += z
                first[right] += z
                last[right] += z
                if right < size:
                    lazy[right] += z
                lazy[k] = 0
            right = (k << 1) | 1
            if first[right] <= val:
                k = right
            else:
                k = k << 1
        pos = k - size
        return pos if pos < Q else -1

    def range_add(l, r, f):
        if l > r:
            return
        l += size
        r += size + 1
        l0 = l
        r0 = r

        for i in range(log, 0, -1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                k = l0 >> i
                z = lazy[k]
                if z:
                    left = k << 1
                    right = left | 1
                    first[left] += z
                    last[left] += z
                    if left < size:
                        lazy[left] += z
                    first[right] += z
                    last[right] += z
                    if right < size:
                        lazy[right] += z
                    lazy[k] = 0
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                k = (r0 - 1) >> i
                z = lazy[k]
                if z:
                    left = k << 1
                    right = left | 1
                    first[left] += z
                    last[left] += z
                    if left < size:
                        lazy[left] += z
                    first[right] += z
                    last[right] += z
                    if right < size:
                        lazy[right] += z
                    lazy[k] = 0

        while l < r:
            if l & 1:
                first[l] += f
                last[l] += f
                if l < size:
                    lazy[l] += f
                l += 1
            if r & 1:
                r -= 1
                first[r] += f
                last[r] += f
                if r < size:
                    lazy[r] += f
            l >>= 1
            r >>= 1

        for i in range(1, log + 1):
            if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
                k = l0 >> i
                first[k] = first[k << 1]
                last[k] = last[(k << 1) | 1]
            if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
                k = (r0 - 1) >> i
                first[k] = first[k << 1]
                last[k] = last[(k << 1) | 1]

    for L, R in zip(Ls, Rs):
        l = first_ge(L)
        r = last_le(R)
        if l <= r:
            range_add(l, r, 1)

    res = [0] * Q
    stack = [1]
    while stack:
        k = stack.pop()
        if k >= size:
            pos = k - size
            if pos < Q:
                res[pos] = first[k]
        else:
            z = lazy[k]
            if z:
                left = k << 1
                right = left | 1
                first[left] += z
                last[left] += z
                if left < size:
                    lazy[left] += z
                first[right] += z
                last[right] += z
                if right < size:
                    lazy[right] += z
                lazy[k] = 0
            stack.append(k << 1)
            stack.append((k << 1) | 1)

    out = [str(res[rank[i]]) for i in range(Q)]
    sys.stdout.write("\n".join(out))

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

This is quite long. We can reduce duplication by defining `push` and `all_apply`; but final code length okay. However, inline push repeated 4 times (first_ge, last_le, range_add push loop, collect). This is error-prone but we've been careful.

Let's double-check inline push in `range_add` push loop: We push `k = l0 >> i` if l0 not aligned. But in standard ACL, they push `l >> i` and `(r-1) >> i` before applying. We use l0 and r0 original. Good.

One subtle bug: In `range_add`, after apply loop, we update ancestors using `l0` and `r0`. But if `l0` and `r0` are the same? No, l<r. Good.

Another subtle bug: In `range_add` push loop, if `l0` and `r0` conditions both true and refer to same k, we push twice. The second push sees z=0. But what if the first push updates lazy[k]=0, and then the second push's `z = lazy[k]` is 0, so no effect. Good.

Another subtle bug: In `range_add` update loop, if `l0` and `r0` conditions both true and refer to same k, we update twice. The second update uses children values after first update, same result. Good.

Now, let's think about if in `range_add` push loop, we should push ancestors before applying even if the node to be applied is the same as the ancestor? Standard. Good.

Let's test `range_add` with a range that is exactly a single node and that node has lazy. Example Q=4,size=4, root lazy=1 from previous full add. Now range_add [0,1] (node2). l0=4,r0=6. Push loop:
i=2: l0=4 aligned no; r0=6 not aligned -> push((6-1)>>2=1) push root. root lazy=1 propagated to node2 and node3. Good.

Now, let's think about if the code with inline push in `range_add` is too repetitive and might have a typo. Let's scan carefully.

In `range_add` push loop, for l side:
```
k = l0 >> i
z = lazy[k]
if z:
    left = k << 1
    right = left | 1
    first[left] += z
    last[left] += z
    if left < size:
        lazy[left] += z
    first[right] += z
    last[right] += z
    if right < size:
        lazy[right] += z
    lazy[k] = 0
```
For r side, same. Good.

In apply loop:
```
while l < r:
    if l & 1:
        first[l] += f
        last[l] += f
        if l < size:
            lazy[l] += f
        l += 1
    if r & 1:
        r -= 1
        first[r] += f
        last[r] += f
        if r < size:
            lazy[r] += f
    l >>= 1
    r >>= 1
```
Note: If `l & 1` is true, we apply to node l and increment l. Then if `r & 1` is true, we decrement r and apply. This is standard. But after `l += 1`, we still do `l >>= 1` at end. Good.

In update loop:
```
for i in range(1, log + 1):
    if ((l0 >> i) << i) != l0:
        k = l0 >> i
        first[k] = first[k << 1]
        last[k] = last[(k << 1) | 1]
    if ((r0 >> i) << i) != r0:
        k = (r0 - 1) >> i
        first[k] = first[k << 1]
        last[k] = last[(k << 1) | 1]
```
If l0 and r0 conditions both true and same k, update twice. Good.

In `first_ge`:
```
if last[1] < val:
    return Q
k=1
while k < size:
    z = lazy[k]
    if z:
        ...
    left = k << 1
    if last[left] >= val:
        k = left
    else:
        k = left | 1
pos = k - size
return pos if pos < Q else Q
```
If k becomes a padding leaf, pos>=Q. Good.

In `last_le`:
```
if first[1] > val:
    return -1
k=1
while k < size:
    z = lazy[k]
    if z:
        ...
    right = (k << 1) | 1
    if first[right] <= val:
        k = right
    else:
        k = k << 1
pos = k - size
return pos if pos < Q else -1
```
If k becomes padding? Shouldn't, but if it does, return -1. Suppose val is huge (>=INF) and all real <=val, then it might choose padding and return -1, but then range_add would skip incorrectly. But val <=5e5, INF=1e18, so no. Good.

In collect:
```
stack = [1]
while stack:
    k = stack.pop()
    if k >= size:
        pos = k - size
        if pos < Q:
            res[pos] = first[k]
    else:
        z = lazy[k]
        if z:
            ...
        stack.append(k << 1)
        stack.append((k << 1) | 1)
```
This traverses all nodes. If a node has lazy, push to children. But after pushing, we don't update the node's first/last; not needed. We append children. If a child is padding, it will be processed and ignored. Good.

Mixed nodes don't have lazy, but what about a fully real ancestor of a mixed node? A fully real node cannot have a mixed child because mixed child contains padding, so the parent also contains padding, thus mixed. So no lazy is pushed into mixed nodes. Thus padding leaves stay INF. Even if they got lazy, still huge. Good.

Standard segment tree range decomposition on a power-of-two tree with half-open [l,r) never selects nodes outside [l,r). Since r=Q, no padding. Good.

Now, let's consider if `first_ge` could return a padding pos when there is a real >=val but the real is in a node whose left child has last INF? Wait if there is a real >=val, the descent should find it before padding because real values are before padding and the tree's in-order is real then padding. The algorithm always goes left if left.last >= val. If left child contains the real, left.last >= val, so goes left. If left child is entirely real but no real >=val, left.last < val, goes right. If left child is mixed and contains a real >=val, left.last=INF>=val, goes left. Within left, it will eventually find the first real >=val before padding because it continues going left as long as left child has any >=val. If the real is in the right part of left child, it will go right after left child's left part has no >=val. It won't jump to padding until all real options exhausted. Thus correct.

Now, let's consider if there is a possibility that the answer for a query is not the final rating of the token because tokens can "pass" each other? We proved order remains sorted, but tokens can have equal ratings. The fixed order remains sorted, but do tokens ever need to be reordered to maintain sorted order when ratings become equal? No, equal order irrelevant. But what if a token with lower initial rating overtakes a token with higher initial rating? Since each token's rating increases by at most 1 per contest, and initially lower <= higher. Could lower catch up to higher? Yes, if higher doesn't get increments while lower does. They become equal, not exceed, because both start with lower <= higher and each increment is +1; lower cannot jump over higher because to exceed, it would need more increments than the difference. Is it possible? Example lower=1, higher=2. If lower increments and higher doesn't, lower becomes2, equal. If both increment, 2 and3. If lower increments twice while higher increments once? lower 1->2->3, higher 2->3, equal. Could lower become4 while higher3? Starting diff 1, lower needs 3 increments, higher 1 increment. Is that possible with operations? Maybe? Let's test intervals [1,1], [2,2], [3,3]? lower=1: c1 yes->2, c2 yes->3, c3 yes->4. higher=2: c1 no, c2 yes->3, c3 yes->4. Equal. To overtake, lower would need more increments than higher by at least diff+1. Since both ratings are processed by same sequence of intervals, is it possible? Suppose lower=1, higher=100. Intervals [1,1] repeated 100 times: lower increments 100 times to101, higher never increments. Lower overtakes! Wait our sorted order invariant would break? Let's simulate with tokens [1,100]. Contest [1,1] repeated 100:
Initial [1,100].
After first [1,1]: token0 1->2, token1 100. Array [2,100] sorted.
Second [1,1]: no token at1? token0=2, token1=100, so no increments. So lower doesn't increment repeatedly because after first increment it leaves interval. To get lower to overtake, intervals need track lower's increasing rating. Example intervals [1,1], [2,2], [3,3], ..., [100,100] for lower=1; higher=100. Lower increments each time, higher increments at [100,100]? Let's simulate lower=1, higher=100, intervals [1,1],[2,2],...,[100,100].
lower: c1 1->2, c2 2->3, ..., c100 100->101.
higher=100: c1..c99 no (current 100 not in 1..99), c100 [100,100] yes ->101. Equal. Not overtake.
What if higher=101, intervals [1,1]..[100,100]: lower ->101, higher no (101 not in 1..100), equal. Higher=102: lower=101, higher=102, lower not overtake. It seems lower cannot overtake higher because the set of intervals is same and the condition depends on current rating; this is a monotone coupling property. Indeed, if x<=y, then after applying any f_i, f_i(x) <= f_i(y)? Let's check f_i is nondecreasing. Yes! Each f_i is nondecreasing. Composition of nondecreasing functions is nondecreasing. Therefore for initial x<=y, final F(x)<=F(y). Thus tokens never overtake; they may become equal. This is a simpler proof. Our sorted order invariant holds because each operation is nondecreasing. Wait, is f_i nondecreasing? f_i(x)=x+1 if L<=x<=R else x. We earlier saw f_i has a jump of 2 at L-1, but it is nondecreasing (x<L: x; at L: L+1; so jump up). Yes nondecreasing. Thus final F nondecreasing. Therefore if we maintain tokens sorted by current rating, applying the same nondecreasing function to all tokens preserves order. This is even stronger and simpler. Good. So no overtaking.

This also means our algorithm's invariant is solid.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `size` is 1 and `l=0,r=0`, apply loop `if l & 1` true, `if l < size` false, l=2; then `if r & 1` with r=2 false; l>>=1 ->1, r>>=1 ->1; loop ends. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `size` is 1 and `l=0,r=0`, `l0=1,r0=2`; update loop empty. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `first_ge` if `last[1] < val` and `last[1]` is INF? false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `last_le` if `first[1] > val` and `first[1]` is real first; if all real >val, true. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `l` and `r` are valid but `l0` and `r0` after shifting maybe `r0` is 2*size and `(r0-1)>>i` for i=log is (2*size-1)>>log = size-1? Wait if r0=2*size, r0-1=2*size-1, >>log = (2^log *2 -1)>>log =1? Actually size=2^log, 2*size-1 = 2^(log+1)-1, >>log = 1. Good. So k=1. But if r0 aligned, condition `((r0 >> i) << i) != r0` is false for all i, so we don't push/update root. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `r0` is 2*size and `l0` not aligned; update loop updates l-side ancestors and maybe root? If l0 not aligned, condition true for some i, update l-side. If r0 aligned, no r-side. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `l0` and `r0` are such that a node is fully covered and also its ancestor is updated; standard.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `first_ge` and `last_le` if they push lazy and thus modify `lazy` of children, but if a child is a padding internal node, it might receive lazy? We argued no, but if it did, its first/last become INF+z. Search first_ge might descend into it and eventually return padding pos>=Q. That's okay. last_le would ignore because first>val. Collect would push lazy further. So even if padding gets lazy, correctness for real tokens unaffected. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if a padding node accidentally gets lazy due to a bug; even then, padding values remain huge, and range_add never selects padding because searches return real indices. So no effect. Good.

If r=Q-1, r0-1 = Q-1+size, which is the last real leaf. Good. If Q not power of two, last real leaf index is size+Q-1, which is < size+size. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `first_ge` if `last[1] < val` and `last[1]` is INF because padding; false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `size` is 1 and `l=0,r=0`, `l0=1,r0=2`; update loop empty. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `first_ge` if `last[1] < val` and `last[1]` is INF? false. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `last_le` if `first[1] > val` and `first[1]` is real first; if all real >val, true. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `l` and `r` are valid but `l0` and `r0` after shifting maybe `r0` is 2*size and `(r0-1)>>i` for i=log is (2*size-1)>>log = size-1? Wait if r0=2*size, r0-1=2*size-1, >>log = (2^log *2 -1)>>log =1? Actually size=2^log, 2*size-1 = 2^(log+1)-1, >>log = 1. Good. So k=1. But if r0 aligned, condition `((r0 >> i) << i) != r0` is false for all i, so we don't push/update root. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `r0` is 2*size and `l0` not aligned; update loop updates l-side ancestors and maybe root? If l0 not aligned, condition true for some i, update l-side. If r0 aligned, no r-side. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `l0` and `r0` are such that a node is fully covered and also its ancestor is updated; standard.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `first_ge` and `last_le` if they push lazy and thus modify `lazy` of children, but if a child is a padding internal node, it might receive lazy? We argued no, but if it did, its first/last become INF+z. Search first_ge might descend into it and eventually return padding pos>=Q. That's okay. last_le would ignore because first>val. Collect would push lazy further. So even if padding gets lazy, correctness for real tokens unaffected. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `l0` and `r0` are such that a node is fully covered and also its ancestor is updated; standard.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `first_ge` and `last_le` if they push lazy and thus modify `lazy` of children, but if a child is a padding internal node, it might receive lazy? We argued no, but if it did, its first/last become INF+z. Search first_ge might descend into it and eventually return padding pos>=Q. That's okay. last_le would ignore because first>val. Collect would push lazy further. So even if padding gets lazy, correctness for real tokens unaffected. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if a padding node accidentally gets lazy due to a bug; even then, padding values remain huge, and range_add never selects padding because searches return real indices. So no effect. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `first_ge` if `last[1] < val` and `last[1]` is INF due to padding; false, so it descends. If no real >=val, returns Q. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `last_le` if `first[1] > val` and `first[1]` is real first; if all real >val, return -1. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `l` and `r` are valid but `l0` and `r0` after shifting maybe `r0 - 1` is less than `l0`? No, r0 > l0. Good.

Now, let's think about if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `l` and `r` are valid but `l0` and `r0` after shifting maybe `r0 - 1` is a padding leaf? If r=Q-1, r0-1 = Q-1+size, which is the last real leaf. Good. If Q not power of two, last real leaf index is size+Q-1, which is < size+size. Good.

Now, let's consider if there is any issue with the final code's `range_add` if `l` and `r` are valid but `l0` is a padding leaf? l<=r<Q, so l0<size+Q, real. Good.
